| **[How to make a moral agent: sentimentalism and rationalism as lenses on AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18714v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can AI systems be moral agents? That is, can they undertake actions that can be correctly assessed as morally right or wrong? There is no settled normative criterion of moral agency that applies neatly outside paradigm human cases. Because of the difficulties with a normative account, we instead propose a research program to explore descriptive moral agency. Most basically, we ask, what are the capabilities we humans possess that underlie our moral agency? To do so, we draw on historical scholarship in philosophy and moral psychology to isolate candidate capabilities. Then we suggest refining candidate capabilities so that they are testable in the context of AI systems. Finally, we offer specific empirical recommendations to test AI systems, especially large language models (LLMs), for those refined capabilities. Our approach offers several benefits. Firstly, it may demonstrate whether the behaviors of AI systems are aligned, or in a broad sense desirable. Secondly, the debate may inform policy questions (e.g., what models should be deployed in which contexts?). Thirdly, our approach may complement engineering and design efforts: when capabilities can be precisely characterized, they can inform the development of models. Fourthly, applying our moral reasoning concepts in the new context of AI systems should improve our understanding of those concepts.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reasoning on Call: Efficient Agentic Robot Control for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26341v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large multimodal models can support long-horizon robot manipulation, but frequent model calls incur substantial latency and token costs. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble. | **[Agentic Scaling of Embodied Tasks in Simulation for Synthetic Data Collection and Policy Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26046v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling embodied interaction data requires a broad collection of usable tasks, yet specifying and validating each task instance remains costly. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/jimmy-zhou/AgenticTask-Kitchen-50 Project website: https://zxl502.github.io/AgenticTask/</p></details> |
| **[The Missing Complement: State-Conditioned Minimal Sufficient Evidence for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20050v3)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval assembles repository context by ranking passages for relevance to the current query. MSS-Complement treats acquisition as set construction, not ranking. Three semantic calls propose a jointly sufficient set, search for what it lacks, and return 4-8 intact source units within 6,144 tokens. One configuration, fixed on calibration data, recovers a complete set for 73.0% of those states at five items and 80.6% at eight, against 61.4% and 72.4% for Qwen3 embedding with reranking. A matched control ranking by similarity alone recovers fewer complete sets, placing the margin over it in the set-level policy, not the computation. The lead persists from frozen repository source with no gold-derived pool. Benchmark and evaluation resources: https://github.com/LordTARN1SHED/SERBench</p></details> |

Applied to a commercial match-3 game, it uncovers patterns invisible to aggregate metrics: a playtesting bot is diverging substantially from humans in certain game modes, while showing no detectable divergence in others. | **[System-One RSI: Building Muscle Memory for Interactive Agents through Guided Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26007v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning models provide strong multimodal task understanding but struggle with sub-second control because deliberation increases decision latency. The high-frequency online inner loop is an Observe, Orient, Decide, Act (OODA) cycle: a decision model maps each visual observation and task question to structured judgments, and a decision tree converts them into timed actions without invoking a reasoning model. The offline outer loop is a recursive improvement cycle carried out by a team of reasoning agents. The team revises the decision-model prompt and decision tree, captures interaction records, derives supervision from those records, and trains or deploys updated model parameters. The improved system then returns to the environment and generates the experience for the next round, consolidating learned distinctions as external muscle memory. The framework operates with a small number of real episodes, without expert demonstrations, environment modification, sandboxing, or parallel simulation. Experiments in Snake, Zenless Zone Zero, and Black Myth: Wukong use a 27B decision model. Mean Snake score increases from 1.30 to 7.35. We present KernelOPT, a profiling-guided multi-agent system for GPU kernel optimization. Four stages of validation combine execution checks, multi-seed numerical comparison, model-level checking with a float64 fallback and end-to-end performance gating ($γ{=}1.03$). The system supports PyTorch nn.Modules, standalone Triton kernels, and Helion kernels. It is evaluated on all 250 KernelBench problems on NVIDIA H200 which includes 100 single-operator problems (Level1), 100 composite operators (Level2), and 50 model architectures (Level3). | **[ArrivalBench: Agent-Generated Data Pipelines Are Correct Once and Wrong Under Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02363v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmarks for agent-generated data work grade a pipeline by running it once against a fixed snapshot. Because the oracle recomputes rather than classifies, a wrong table and a crash are distinct verdicts: a crash is visible to monitoring a team already runs, and a wrong table is not. On 40 tasks we built, our reimplementation of single-execution grading certifies 86-100% of the pipelines eleven models produce; re-executing the same artifacts finds 7.0-79.2% of the certified ones silently wrong. The gap is not produced by the repair loop: within the same model and task, pipelines repaired against the snapshot test fail replay about as often as those that passed it first time. In every model, idempotency hazards fail more often than ordering hazards. Separating a wrong answer from a crash also changes how interventions read: a hazard warning cuts one model's silent failure from 48.2% to 10.5% while raising its crash rate from 9.0% to 37.0%, so all-in failure moves only from 51.0% to 44.0%. | **[CRAFT: Analog Circuit Design by Reasoning LLM Agents from Pre-Computed Lookup Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25908v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated analog circuit sizing methods, from black-box optimizers to recent large language model (LLM) agents, typically search the design space with a SPICE simulator in the loop, whereas an experienced designer works out a design by hand analysis. | **[Validation-driven Deployment of LLM Inference Platforms for Agentic Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25905v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprises are moving agentic workloads from closed APIs to their own open-weight model deployments. | **[Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25898v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.</p></details> |  |

We instead treat memory as the assistant's belief about the user. Following Bourdieu's account of habitus as durable but revisable dispositions, we model the user as a latent preference state that every interaction, including the current request, both reveals and updates. The belief summarizes what the history supports about this state, and memory consists of the individual beliefs that bear on the agent's current decision, each with its evidence, scope, and status. We implement this view in HabitusMem, a trained writer that states these beliefs as text records for a frozen agent. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. All 31,800 trials on local copies of deployed contract code match the derivation. A controlled comparison shows that committing the terms in the signed nonce binds the order. When the facilitator submits a payment to a merchant account without code, binding the facilitator requires a separate recipient contract. Of 26,653 sampled USD Coin authorizations redeemed on Base, 98.7% followed a path that any holder of the signature could redeem. We prove sufficient conditions for a recipient contract to enforce the signed mandate under a trusted signer and show which recipient a wallet displays. To fill this gap, we present {\setlength{\fboxsep}{1pt}\href{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DS2Agent-9FE8/}{\fcolorbox{blue}{white}{DS2Agent}}}, an open-source framework that constructs interactive agents from device datasheets. Handling these problems involves on average 4.3 further user turns and 12.71 minutes of recorded response and execution time, and users express dissatisfaction more often. On held-out recordings, the fine-tuned model separates turn ends from pauses better than both text baselines and is the best calibrated of the three. Timing features of the current speaker are similarly informative, and their combination with the fine-tuned text score gives the best timing-based results; with logistic regression, this combination also outperforms the same combination with either text baseline. In a latency simulation, adding the text score to a silence timeout shortens the mean response delay at the same interruption rate. On automatic Whisper transcripts, the fine-tuned model retains its advantage over the zero-shot model.</p></details> |  |
| **[ToolPulse: Side-Channel Eavesdropping on Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25470v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that translate user requests into sequences of local tool invocations. While prior cache-based side-channel attacks target the locally deployed models, they typically require the adversary to share model-related components or hardware resources with the victim's inference process. These assumptions do not apply to the predominant agentic settings, in which commercial LLMs execute on vendor-controlled infrastructure. We show that agentic execution exposes a more practical attack surface: although inference remains remote, tools are executed on the user's machine and commonly rely on shared system libraries. In this paper, we first systematically evaluate Flush+Reload and Prime+Probe for monitoring agentic tool invocations. Our evaluation across three hardware platforms shows that Flush+Reload enables accurate, real-time tool monitoring, whereas Prime+Probe, despite supporting cross-VM attacks, incurs prohibitive monitoring overhead and suffers from cache-set aliasing. We present ToolPulse, a cache-based side-channel eavesdropping framework against tool-using agents. We first design the Tool Recovery Attack to reconstruct an agent's tool-execution workflow. We evaluate the Tool Recovery Attack on 11 LLMs across Codex and Claude Code. It consistently reconstructs tool workflows, with the lowest F1 score exceeding 93%. We further demonstrate two attack scenarios enabled by the recovered traces. Task Profiling Attack infers user intent across two Terminal-Bench task categories and the Trigger Localization Attack identifies sensitive tool calls in encryption/decryption and model training/inference workloads. Even under substantial system noise on a server shared by approximately 20 users, both Task Profiling Attack and Trigger Localization Attack maintain high accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[Look Closer, Look Back, or Wait for Agentic Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24267v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming video models must answer questions about a live video under strict memory and latency limits. Feeding only the latest few frames keeps present perception sharp, whereas adding past frames dilutes attention on the current scene and triggers hallucinations. Recent cascade methods retrieve past frames when uncertain, but they still fail when current frames are blurry or when decisive future clues have not appeared yet. We introduce ActiveForager, a streaming video agent that maintains a clean short working window and gathers extra evidence only on demand. For each query, the agent either answers directly, zooms into high-resolution recent frames, recalls past events from memory, or waits for upcoming frames. When recalling history, a shared-weight sub-agent verifies candidate clips in isolation and returns a compact evidence summary. A stream-decoupled attention mask then keeps historical evidence from interfering with present visual tokens. We train this policy using counterfactual information gain, which rewards a tool call only when the gathered evidence makes the true answer more likely and prevents tool collapse across all actions. Across Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B on OVO-Bench, ActiveForager improves real-time perception, historical recall, and proactive response at once, lifting overall accuracy on Qwen3-VL-8B from 47.6% to 65.9%. It also doubles zero-shot accuracy on the multi-hop Video-Holmes benchmark while adding minimal visual tokens per query.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent-Centric Observation Adaptation for Robust Visual Control under Dynamic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24661v5)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual control maps visual streams to actions in reinforcement learning and vision-language-action (VLA) policies. Changing weather, noise, blur, and compression can obscure different task cues within one episode. Existing control benchmarks mainly test scene distractions, while generic restorers optimize whole-image fidelity. We introduce the Visual Degraded Control Suite (VDCS) to evaluate seven degradations under Markov switching. We propose Agent-Centric Observations with Mixture-of-Experts (ACO-MoE), which routes each frame to a repair expert, restores RGB content, and composes task-relevant foregrounds. On eight VDCS tasks, corruption lowers DreamerV3's mean return from 769 to 562. ACO-MoE raises it to 730, recovering 81.2% of the lost return. It also improves returns on DMC-GB and RoboSuite and retains 70% to 97% of clean-input mean return under six unseen perturbation families. We train ACO-MoE on robot demonstrations and deploy it with a 3B-parameter VLA policy on a Piper arm for cube placement in blue and white boxes and cup placement on a coaster. On the blue-box and cup tasks, motion blur and physical low light with added noise reduce success from 100% to at most 10%. ACO-MoE raises success to 75% to 95% over 20 trials per task and condition.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, including supplementary material. Zhengru Fang and Yu Guo contributed equally. Source code is available at https://github.com/fangzr/aco-moe-code</p></details> |
| **[Revisiting the Survival of Agent-Attributed Code: How Measurement Choices Decide the Verdict](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21784v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Studies of how long code from AI coding agents survives after it is merged reach conflicting verdicts: agent code is reported to be retained longer than human code, about as long, or modified sooner. Each study makes defensible measurement choices, but how much these choices decide the verdict is unknown. We revisit the survival of agent-attributed code in our earlier study: 854,139 lines that 2,802 merged pull requests (PRs) added to 215 repositories of the AIDev dataset. We vary nine measurement choices in five research questions and build a specification curve of 88 survival analyses, tested under three variance estimators, several observation windows, and permuted labels. For code from human-supervised agents, mostly GitHub Copilot, the choices change the size of the effect but not its direction. For code from the fully-autonomous agent Devin, they decide the verdict. Across repositories, 15 of 22 specifications report it modified sooner when lines are treated as independent, but only 3 when standard errors are clustered by repository, no more than permuted labels produce. Within repositories, no specification detects a difference. LLM intent classifiers agree with human labels about as well as two humans agree with each other, whereas keyword rules do not (kappa = 0.69 to 0.72 against 0.38), and the two reach different verdicts on why agent code is modified. Humans make most modifications to agent code under every unit and window. Re-run on our data, five published designs each change their result with a single choice. Whether agent code survives longer than human code is therefore decided as much by the measurement as by the code. We derive an operationalization checklist for post-merge studies.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evolve Where It Matters: Coverage-Guided Self-Evolution of Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25429v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent skills package procedural knowledge as natural-language instructions, and a skill helps only when its instructions are correct and properly followed by the agent. Self-evolving agents revise their own skills from the runs they make, but existing methods judge each run as a whole, by its outcome or by where it went wrong, and cannot tell whether a failure comes from a wrong instruction or from one the agent did not follow. Instruction-level coverage was introduced to test skills and labels each instruction of a run as follow, not-follow, or not covered, which separates these causes. We leverage it for skill self-evolution with SkillPlumber, a framework in which an agent evolves its own skills where coverage shows they fall short. A persistent root cause analysis agent turns each label into a revision question and checks each revision against the next run, while a maintainer distills investigations across tasks into a shared strategy library. Across three benchmarks and three executors, SkillPlumber solves 64.7% to 70.5% of the tasks, 25.6 to 30.4 percentage points higher than the initial skill and 8.7 to 14.0 percentage points higher than the strongest baseline, and removing coverage reduces recovered failures by 20.8% on SkillsBench with GPT-5.5.</p></details> | 15 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Toward a Science of AI Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25404v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are rapidly evolving from isolated personal assistants into networked actors that interact with one another at scale. We envision the emergence of AI agent societies, with their own social and economic dynamics, as a new research frontier. We argue that AI agent societies should be studied as a distinct object of inquiry: neither simply larger collections of individual agents nor merely simulations of human society. To formalize this perspective, we propose four core properties that a valid AI agent society should satisfy: individualized objectives, rules and governance, autonomy, and scale and complexity. Building on this framework, we identify four classes of societal behaviors worth studying in AI agent societies: economic behaviors, behaviors under conflict-of-interest, unsafe and unethical behaviors, and system-level behaviors. We then outline key technical challenges--including property parameterization, parameter balancing, and robust implementation--and argue that progress on these challenges could enable scientifically informative and practically useful models of AI agent societies. Finally, we revisit existing multi-agent systems through the lens of the proposed core properties, show that they instantiate only subsets of them, and discuss implications for platform design, evaluation, and governance.</p></details> | <details><summary>KDD 2...</summary><p>KDD 2026 Blue Sky Ideas Track</p></details> |
| **[From Reflection to Co-Evolution: Self-Evolving Agents with Locally Verified Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10382v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents have made impressive progress in coding and mathematical reasoning, motivating growing interest in learning from long-horizon interaction. Memory supports this process, but plausible lessons can be redundant or reinforce inappropriate actions. Can a memory's source decision provide a practical test for whether to retain it? We localize consequential trajectory steps, extract candidate memories, and replay each source decision with and without its candidate under the same context. This reveals two properties: most candidates barely shift the probed distribution, and both successful and failed source actions can be reinforced or suppressed. These observations motivate Anchor, which tests shift magnitude and source-role alignment before retention. Admitted memories guide later trajectories, enabling experience-driven improvement under a frozen policy. On held-out ALFWorld and WebShop tasks, Anchor improves success rate and mean score by up to 19.9% and 17.2%, respectively, relative to the strongest baseline for each backbone. With Qwen3.5-27B, it retains 41% fewer ALFWorld cards than unfiltered retention and outperforms all four WebShop cost-comparison baselines while using fewer tokens.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 9 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Weight Updates: Evolving Agent Harnesses for Domain Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25380v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Domain specialization of LLM agents is usually framed as a weight-update problem: collect trajectories, run reinforcement learning (RL) or supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and deploy a new checkpoint. We study a complementary axis: evolving the \textbf{agent harness}---the system prompt, tool schemas, skill library, and planning scaffolding---while keeping the policy frozen. We cast harness evolution as an RL-style outer loop and instantiate it with GEPA, a reflective evolutionary optimizer. The resulting harness adapters require no GPU training, are loadable per vertical domain, and compose with weight-based RL. Experiments with Codev, a Claude Code-style coding agent, on SWE-bench Verified, OfficeBench, and Terminal-Bench show a consistent pattern: weight updates give the strongest single-path gains, harness evolution recovers most of those gains at a small fraction of the compute, and combining both paths is best overall. We also analyze a failure mode of SFT-based specialization---shortened reasoning chains and increased truncation---and argue that harness adapters avoid it by construction. Our results position harness evolution as a practical, non-destructive complement to weight updates for vertical agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Stop Going in Circles:Loop-Triggered Routing for System 1 Decision Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25363v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>System 1 decision models (represented by Jev) provide fast, low-cost action selection, but existing agent integrations do not reliably translate these local advantages into task completion. At certain points during execution, an agent may repeatedly select the same actions without making progress toward its goal. LLM-based policies offer strong reasoning capabilities that support planning within an agent, but invoking them at every step incurs substantial overhead. The effective coordination should therefore preserve Jev's fast local action selection while introducing LLM reasoning when execution stalls. We observe that recurring state-action pairs can signal stalled execution. We therefore propose LoopRoute, a simple but effective action router that uses this signal to allocate control within an agent. The decision model acts by default. When a loop is detected, the LLM provides brief, feedback-driven assistance and then returns control to the decision model. Later loops can request further assistance. The rule requires neither additional training nor model-specific confidence calibration. Across three benchmarks and seven LLMs, LoopRoute improves task success over standalone Jev with only limited use of the LLM policy. The mean gains are 21.0%, 60.1%, and 30.1% on ALFWorld, MapReader, and TextWorld cooking, respectively. Compared with full LLM control, LoopRoute also substantially reduces the mean cumulative API time and API-call cost per task. The same rule also improves the success rates of six frozen decision models.</p></details> | 15 pages,3 figures |
| **[AppSci-Bench: Can LLMs Build AI Agents and Make Them Better?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25360v1)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used not only to power AI agents but also to build them. While many benchmarks assess the quality of LLM- powered agents, few ascertain how good LLMs are at building them. To this end, we introduce AppSci-Bench, where we construct a setting akin to how human Applied Scientists build agents for businesses - through eliciting task requirements from stakeholders, exploring the business's infrastructure, and scientifically improve the target agent by designing experiments and internal benchmarks. AppSci-Bench features seven realistic domains - five new ones (Slides Generation, Order-to-Cash, Compliance Review, Customer Call Insights, and CRM Insights) and two domains recast from tau3-bench (Yao et al., 2024; Shi et al., 2026b)(Airline and Banking) - each with a held-out evaluation suite customized for that task. We evaluate seven frontier models and find AppSci-Bench challenging: the best model, Claude Opus 5.0, only reaches a mean-minimum score of 59.1% across 3 build attempts; other models do significantly worse. Models struggle with eliciting task requirements (only 65% for Opus 5.0), build representative internal evaluations (models saturate their own evaluation 65% of the time but fail to solve the held-out task), presenting challenges for future research. The benchmark code is available here: https://github.com/sanjari-orb/appsci-bench.</p></details> |  |
